People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

Well… I guess I came in because I just can’t seem to settle down inside myself anymore. It’s like I’m always needing to get up and do the next thing, even when I’m tired and I know I ought to just sit still. I don’t know if that makes sense, but it’s been bothering me.

Even at church meetings, when I know I’m supposed to just sit quietly, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot or rubbing the edge of my purse, or shifting around in the chair. I try to be discreet about it, but sometimes the ladies beside me start looking over, and then I feel embarrassed. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do certain things in a specific way or repeat certain actions to feel okay?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do certain things in a specific way or repeat certain actions to feel okay?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. At least I haven’t noticed anything like that. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of restlessness and inability to settle down interfering with your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say it doesn’t, but… yes, I think it has. My family has had to help me more with bills and paperwork because I get unsettled and don’t finish things the way I should. And at church or in the garden group, there have been times people had to redo plans or cover something I said I’d handle and then dropped.\n\nI’m embarrassed saying that, because I do care about those groups very much. I don’t want people thinking I’m unreliable, but I think some of them don’t feel they can count on me like they used to. Is that the kind of interference you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or distressed when you're unable to engage in these activities or move around?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I feel a little silly saying it that way. It’s not that I’m trying to be difficult. I just feel like I can’t settle inside my own skin anymore sometimes, like I need to jump up and do the next thing even when I’m tired and should just be sitting still.\n\nIf I can’t get up or move around, I get uncomfortable and sort of distressed inside. I try to hide it, but it’s there. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to perform specific rituals or behaviors to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I have anything like that. I may double-check things now and then, but not because I think something terrible will happen if I don’t do it a certain way.\n\nI don’t have rituals, at least not that I’m aware of. If I’m misunderstanding what you mean, you can tell me, but I don’t think that’s really me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I don’t like to think of myself that way. It’s more that in conversations, especially committee meetings, a thought pops into my head and I’m afraid I’ll lose it, so I just say it.\n\nSometimes I realize afterward that I interrupted someone who was still talking. I don’t mean to be rude at all, and I feel badly when I notice it. But yes, I do blurt things out before I’ve really thought through whether it’s the right moment. Is that the kind of impulsive thing you’re asking about?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of restlessness and inability to settle down interfering with your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say it doesn’t, but… yes, I think it has. My family has had to help me more with bills and paperwork because I get unsettled and don’t finish things the way I should. And at church or in the garden group, there have been times people had to redo plans or cover something I said I’d handle and then dropped.\n\nI’m embarrassed saying that, because I do care about those groups very much. I don’t want people thinking I’m unreliable, but I think some of them don’t feel they can count on me like they used to. Is that the kind of interference you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or distressed when you're unable to engage in these activities or move around?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I feel a little silly saying it that way. It’s not that I’m trying to be difficult. I just feel like I can’t settle inside my own skin anymore sometimes, like I need to jump up and do the next thing even when I’m tired and should just be sitting still.\n\nIf I can’t get up or move around, I get uncomfortable and sort of distressed inside. I try to hide it, but it’s there. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to perform specific rituals or behaviors to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I have anything like that. I may double-check things now and then, but not because I think something terrible will happen if I don’t do it a certain way.\n\nI don’t have rituals, at least not that I’m aware of. If I’m misunderstanding what you mean, you can tell me, but I don’t think that’s really me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I don’t like to think of myself that way. It’s more that in conversations, especially committee meetings, a thought pops into my head and I’m afraid I’ll lose it, so I just say it.\n\nSometimes I realize afterward that I interrupted someone who was still talking. I don’t mean to be rude at all, and I feel badly when I notice it. But yes, I do blurt things out before I’ve really thought through whether it’s the right moment. Is that the kind of impulsive thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need to repeat certain actions or tasks over and over?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Not in the sense of having to repeat something over and over until it feels right, or because I’m afraid something will happen.\n\nI may go back to something if I forgot what I was doing, but that feels different to me. I don’t have a strong need to repeat actions like a ritual, at least not that I’ve noticed. Does that answer it okay?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t know if that makes sense, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts or ideas that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so, not in the way you’re asking, I don’t believe. I don’t know if that makes sense, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts or ideas that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so, not in the way you’re asking, I don’t believe. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do certain things in a specific way or repeat certain actions to feel okay?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. I try to be careful, I really do, but I still make these little mistakes that end up causing a bigger problem.\n\nLike I’ll write the wrong amount in the garden checkbook, or I’ll leave someone’s name off the church supper list even though I honestly thought I had checked it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s more restlessness than having extra energy. I don’t feel energetic, really… sometimes I’m actually worn out.

It’s more like I can’t settle inside my own skin, like I ought to be able to sit still but something in me keeps saying, “Get up, do the next thing.” I know that may sound a little silly, but that’s the best way I can describe it.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I came in because I just can’t seem to settle down inside myself anymore. It’s like I’m always needing to get up and do the next thing, even when I’m tired and I know I ought to just sit still. I don’t know if that makes sense, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts or ideas that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so, not in the way you’re asking, I don’t believe. I mean, I worry about things like anyone does, and sometimes something will be on my mind, but I wouldn’t say I have the same thought repeating over and over that I can’t get rid of. At least, I haven’t noticed that. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or having trouble sitting still in situations where you're expected to remain seated?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, more than I’d like to admit. Even at church meetings, when I know I’m supposed to just sit quietly, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot or rubbing the edge of my purse, or shifting around in the chair. I try to be discreet about it, but sometimes the ladies beside me start looking over, and then I feel embarrassed. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do certain things in a specific way or repeat certain actions to feel okay?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. I mean, I like things done a certain way sometimes, but I don’t feel like I have to repeat something over and over or do it just right before I can feel okay. At least I haven’t noticed anything like that. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of restlessness and inability to settle down interfering with your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say it doesn’t, but… yes, I think it has. My family has had to help me more with bills and paperwork because I get unsettled and don’t finish things the way I should. And at church or in the garden group, there have been times people had to redo plans or cover something I said I’d handle and then dropped.\n\nI’m embarrassed saying that, because I do care about those groups very much. I don’t want people thinking I’m unreliable, but I think some of them don’t feel they can count on me like they used to. Is that the kind of interference you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or distressed when you're unable to engage in these activities or move around?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I feel a little silly saying it that way. It’s not that I’m trying to be difficult. I just feel like I can’t settle inside my own skin anymore sometimes, like I need to jump up and do the next thing even when I’m tired and should just be sitting still.\n\nIf I can’t get up or move around, I get uncomfortable and sort of distressed inside. I try to hide it, but it’s there. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to perform specific rituals or behaviors to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I have anything like that. I may double-check things now and then, but not because I think something terrible will happen if I don’t do it a certain way.\n\nI don’t have rituals, at least not that I’m aware of. If I’m misunderstanding what you mean, you can tell me, but I don’t think that’s really me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I don’t like to think of myself that way. It’s more that in conversations, especially committee meetings, a thought pops into my head and I’m afraid I’ll lose it, so I just say it.\n\nSometimes I realize afterward that I interrupted someone who was still talking. I don’t mean to be rude at all, and I feel badly when I notice it. But yes, I do blurt things out before I’ve really thought through whether it’s the right moment. Is that the kind of impulsive thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need to repeat certain actions or tasks over and over?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Not in the sense of having to repeat something over and over until it feels right, or because I’m afraid something will happen.\n\nI may go back to something if I forgot what I was doing, but that feels different to me. I don’t have a strong need to repeat actions like a ritual, at least not that I’ve noticed. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes in your daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’m afraid I do, more than I’d like to admit. I wish I could say it was only when I’m busy or around certain people, but it feels more inside me than outside.\n\nThat’s actually part of why I came in. I feel like I can’t settle in my own skin anymore, like I need to jump up and do the next thing even when I’m tired and should be able to just sit still. I’m not sure I’d call them strong emotions, exactly. I mean… I can get a little frustrated if I feel stuck sitting still, but I don’t know that it turns into anything big.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I came in because I just can’t seem to settle down inside myself anymore. It’s like I’m always needing to get up and do the next thing, even when I’m tired and I know I ought to just sit still. I don’t know if that makes sense, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts or ideas that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so, not in the way you’re asking, I don’t believe. I mean, I worry about things like anyone does, and sometimes something will be on my mind, but I wouldn’t say I have the same thought repeating over and over that I can’t get rid of. At least, I haven’t noticed that. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or having trouble sitting still in situations where you're expected to remain seated?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, more than I’d like to admit. Even at church meetings, when I know I’m supposed to just sit quietly, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot or rubbing the edge of my purse, or shifting around in the chair. I try to be discreet about it, but sometimes the ladies beside me start looking over, and then I feel embarrassed. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do certain things in a specific way or repeat certain actions to feel okay?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. I mean, I like things done a certain way sometimes, but I don’t feel like I have to repeat something over and over or do it just right before I can feel okay. At least I haven’t noticed anything like that. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of restlessness and inability to settle down interfering with your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say it doesn’t, but… yes, I think it has. My family has had to help me more with bills and paperwork because I get unsettled and don’t finish things the way I should. And at church or in the garden group, there have been times people had to redo plans or cover something I said I’d handle and then dropped.\n\nI’m embarrassed saying that, because I do care about those groups very much. I don’t want people thinking I’m unreliable, but I think some of them don’t feel they can count on me like they used to. Is that the kind of interference you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or distressed when you're unable to engage in these activities or move around?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I feel a little silly saying it that way. It’s not that I’m trying to be difficult. I just feel like I can’t settle inside my own skin anymore sometimes, like I need to jump up and do the next thing even when I’m tired and should just be sitting still.\n\nIf I can’t get up or move around, I get uncomfortable and sort of distressed inside. I try to hide it, but it’s there. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to perform specific rituals or behaviors to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I have anything like that. I may double-check things now and then, but not because I think something terrible will happen if I don’t do it a certain way.\n\nI don’t have rituals, at least not that I’m aware of. If I’m misunderstanding what you mean, you can tell me, but I don’t think that’s really me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I don’t like to think of myself that way. It’s more that in conversations, especially committee meetings, a thought pops into my head and I’m afraid I’ll lose it, so I just say it.\n\nSometimes I realize afterward that I interrupted someone who was still talking. I don’t mean to be rude at all, and I feel badly when I notice it. But yes, I do blurt things out before I’ve really thought through whether it’s the right moment. Is that the kind of impulsive thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need to repeat certain actions or tasks over and over?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Not in the sense of having to repeat something over and over until it feels right, or because I’m afraid something will happen.\n\nI may go back to something if I forgot what I was doing, but that feels different to me. I don’t have a strong need to repeat actions like a ritual, at least not that I’ve noticed. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes in your daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’m afraid I do, more than I’d like to admit. I try to be careful, I really do, but I still make these little mistakes that end up causing a bigger problem.\n\nLike I’ll write the wrong amount in the garden checkbook, or I’ll leave someone’s name off the church supper list even though I honestly thought I had checked it. Then later I feel awful, because it looks careless, and I don’t want people to think I don’t care.\n\nSo yes, I guess paying close attention to the details is hard for me sometimes. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these feelings of restlessness and inability to settle down in all areas of your life, or are they mostly limited to specific situations or environments?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s not just one place, I don’t think. I wish I could say it was only when I’m busy or around certain people, but it feels more inside me than outside.\n\nThat’s actually part of why I came in. I feel like I can’t settle in my own skin anymore, like I need to jump up and do the next thing even when I’m tired and should be able to just sit still. I try to make it sound normal, like I’m just keeping busy, but it doesn’t always feel normal to me.\n\nDoes that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is that you can't focus or pay attention, or that you just can't seem to settle down and be still?\n\nPatient: I think… if I’m being honest, it’s more the not being able to settle down. I do have trouble focusing sometimes, but the thing that really made me come in is this feeling like I can’t settle inside my own skin anymore.\n\nIt’s like I have to jump up and do the next thing, even when I’m worn out and I know I should just be sitting still. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but that’s the part that worries me most. Is that answering what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your trouble with settling down is more related to feeling restless or having too much energy?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more restlessness than having extra energy. I don’t feel energetic, really… sometimes I’m actually worn out.\n\nIt’s more like I can’t settle inside my own skin, like I ought to be able to sit still but something in me keeps saying, “Get up, do the next thing.” I know that may sound a little silly, but that’s the best way I can describe it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often experience strong emotions, such as irritability or frustration, when you're unable to engage in activities or move around?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them strong emotions, exactly. I mean… I can get a little frustrated if I feel stuck sitting still, but I don’t know that it turns into anything big.\n\nIt’s more uncomfortable than angry, if that makes sense. Like I’m aware I want to move or do something, and then I feel bothered by not being able to. But I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I think… yes, maybe not as much as the restlessness, but it does bother me more than I like to admit.

I’ll think I’ve checked something, and then I’ve still missed a little detail. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I came in because I just can’t seem to settle down inside myself anymore. It’s like I’m always needing to get up and do the next thing, even when I’m tired and I know I ought to just sit still. I don’t know if that makes sense, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts or ideas that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so, not in the way you’re asking, I don’t believe. I mean, I worry about things like anyone does, and sometimes something will be on my mind, but I wouldn’t say I have the same thought repeating over and over that I can’t get rid of. At least, I haven’t noticed that. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or having trouble sitting still in situations where you're expected to remain seated?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, more than I’d like to admit. Even at church meetings, when I know I’m supposed to just sit quietly, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot or rubbing the edge of my purse, or shifting around in the chair. I try to be discreet about it, but sometimes the ladies beside me start looking over, and then I feel embarrassed. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do certain things in a specific way or repeat certain actions to feel okay?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. I mean, I like things done a certain way sometimes, but I don’t feel like I have to repeat something over and over or do it just right before I can feel okay. At least I haven’t noticed anything like that. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of restlessness and inability to settle down interfering with your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say it doesn’t, but… yes, I think it has. My family has had to help me more with bills and paperwork because I get unsettled and don’t finish things the way I should. And at church or in the garden group, there have been times people had to redo plans or cover something I said I’d handle and then dropped.\n\nI’m embarrassed saying that, because I do care about those groups very much. I don’t want people thinking I’m unreliable, but I think some of them don’t feel they can count on me like they used to. Is that the kind of interference you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or distressed when you're unable to engage in these activities or move around?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I feel a little silly saying it that way. It’s not that I’m trying to be difficult. I just feel like I can’t settle inside my own skin anymore sometimes, like I need to jump up and do the next thing even when I’m tired and should just be sitting still.\n\nIf I can’t get up or move around, I get uncomfortable and sort of distressed inside. I try to hide it, but it’s there. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to perform specific rituals or behaviors to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I have anything like that. I may double-check things now and then, but not because I think something terrible will happen if I don’t do it a certain way.\n\nI don’t have rituals, at least not that I’m aware of. If I’m misunderstanding what you mean, you can tell me, but I don’t think that’s really me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I don’t like to think of myself that way. It’s more that in conversations, especially committee meetings, a thought pops into my head and I’m afraid I’ll lose it, so I just say it.\n\nSometimes I realize afterward that I interrupted someone who was still talking. I don’t mean to be rude at all, and I feel badly when I notice it. But yes, I do blurt things out before I’ve really thought through whether it’s the right moment. Is that the kind of impulsive thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need to repeat certain actions or tasks over and over?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Not in the sense of having to repeat something over and over until it feels right, or because I’m afraid something will happen.\n\nI may go back to something if I forgot what I was doing, but that feels different to me. I don’t have a strong need to repeat actions like a ritual, at least not that I’ve noticed. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes in your daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’m afraid I do, more than I’d like to admit. I try to be careful, I really do, but I still make these little mistakes that end up causing a bigger problem.\n\nLike I’ll write the wrong amount in the garden checkbook, or I’ll leave someone’s name off the church supper list even though I honestly thought I had checked it. Then later I feel awful, because it looks careless, and I don’t want people to think I don’t care.\n\nSo yes, I guess paying close attention to the details is hard for me sometimes. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these feelings of restlessness and inability to settle down in all areas of your life, or are they mostly limited to specific situations or environments?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s not just one place, I don’t think. I wish I could say it was only when I’m busy or around certain people, but it feels more inside me than outside.\n\nThat’s actually part of why I came in. I feel like I can’t settle in my own skin anymore, like I need to jump up and do the next thing even when I’m tired and should be able to just sit still. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes in your daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’m afraid I do, more than I’d like to admit. I try to be careful, I really do, but I still make these little mistakes that end up causing a bigger problem.\n\nLike I’ll write the wrong amount in the garden checkbook, or I’ll leave someone’s name off the church supper list even though I honestly thought I had checked it. Then later I feel awful, because it looks careless, and I don’t want people to think I don’t care.\n\nSo yes, I guess paying close attention to the details is hard for me sometimes. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these feelings of restlessness and inability to settle down in all areas of your life, or are they mostly limited to specific situations or environments?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s not just one place, I don’t think. I wish I could say it was only when I’m busy or around certain people, but it feels more inside me than outside.\n\nThat’s actually part of why I came in. I feel like I can’t settle in my own skin anymore, like I need to jump up and do the next thing even when I’m tired and should be able to just sit still. I try to make it sound normal, like I’m just keeping busy, but it doesn’t always feel normal to me.\n\nDoes that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is that you can't focus or pay attention, or that you just can't seem to settle down and be still?\n\nPatient: I think… if I’m being honest, it’s more the not being able to settle down. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but that’s the part that worries me most. Is that answering what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your trouble with settling down is more related to feeling restless or having too much energy?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more restlessness than having extra energy. I don’t feel energetic, really… sometimes I’m actually worn out.\n\nIt’s more like I can’t settle inside my own skin, like I ought to be able to sit still but something in me keeps saying, “Get up, do the next thing.” I know that may sound a little silly, but that’s the best way I can describe it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often experience strong emotions, such as irritability or frustration, when you're unable to engage in activities or move around?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them strong emotions, exactly. I mean… I can get a little frustrated if I feel stuck sitting still, but I don’t know that it turns into anything big.\n\nIt’s more uncomfortable than angry, if that makes sense. Like I’m aware I want to move or do something, and then I feel bothered by not being able to. But I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your trouble paying attention to details is as bothersome to you as your restlessness and inability to settle down?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, maybe not as much as the restlessness, but it does bother me more than I like to admit.\n\nI’ll think I’ve checked something, and then I’ve still missed a little detail. Like with the garden club checkbook, I wrote the wrong amount once, and it turned into a bigger mess than it should have. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or having trouble sitting still in situations where you're expected to remain seated?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, more than I’d like to admit. Even at church meetings, when I know I’m supposed to just sit quietly, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot or rubbing the edge of my purse, or shifting around in the chair. I try to be discreet about it, but sometimes the ladies beside me start looking over, and then I feel embarrassed. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do certain things in a specific way or repeat certain actions to feel okay?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. I mean, I like things done a certain way sometimes, but I don’t feel like I have to repeat something over and over or do it just right before I can feel okay. At least I haven’t noticed anything like that. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of restlessness and inability to settle down interfering with your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say it doesn’t, but… yes, I think it has. My family has had to help me more with bills and paperwork because I get unsettled and don’t finish things the way I should. And at church or in the garden group, there have been times people had to redo plans or cover something I said I’d handle and then dropped.\n\nI’m embarrassed saying that, because I do care about those groups very much. I don’t want people thinking I’m unreliable, but I think some of them don’t feel they can count on me like they used to. Is that the kind of interference you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or distressed when you're unable to engage in these activities or move around?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I feel a little silly saying it that way. It’s not that I’m trying to be difficult. I just feel like I can’t settle inside my own skin anymore sometimes, like I need to jump up and do the next thing even when I’m tired and should just be sitting still.\n\nIf I can’t get up or move around, I get uncomfortable and sort of distressed inside. I try to hide it, but it’s there. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to perform specific rituals or behaviors to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I have anything like that. I may double-check things now and then, but not because I think something terrible will happen if I don’t do it a certain way.\n\nI don’t have rituals, at least not that I’m aware of. If I’m misunderstanding what you mean, you can tell me, but I don’t think that’s really me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I don’t like to think of myself that way. It’s more that in conversations, especially committee meetings, a thought pops into my head and I’m afraid I’ll lose it, so I just say it.\n\nSometimes I realize afterward that I interrupted someone who was still talking. I don’t mean to be rude at all, and I feel badly when I notice it. But yes, I do blurt things out before I’ve really thought through whether it’s the right moment. Is that the kind of impulsive thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need to repeat certain actions or tasks over and over?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Not in the sense of having to repeat something over and over until it feels right, or because I’m afraid something will happen.\n\nI may go back to something if I forgot what I was doing, but that feels different to me. I don’t have a strong need to repeat actions like a ritual, at least not that I’ve noticed. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes in your daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’m afraid I do, more than I’d like to admit. I try to be careful, I really do, but I still make these little mistakes that end up causing a bigger problem.\n\nLike I’ll write the wrong amount in the garden checkbook, or I’ll leave someone’s name off the church supper list even though I honestly thought I had checked it. Then later I feel awful, because it looks careless, and I don’t want people to think I don’t care.\n\nSo yes, I guess paying close attention to the details is hard for me sometimes. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these feelings of restlessness and inability to settle down in all areas of your life, or are they mostly limited to specific situations or environments?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s not just one place, I don’t think. I wish I could say it was only when I’m busy or around certain people, but it feels more inside me than outside.\n\nThat’s actually part of why I came in. I feel like I can’t settle in my own skin anymore, like I need to jump up and do the next thing even when I’m tired and should be able to just sit still. I try to make it sound normal, like I’m just keeping busy, but it doesn’t always feel normal to me.\n\nDoes that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is that you can't focus or pay attention, or that you just can't seem to settle down and be still?\n\nPatient: I think… if I’m being honest, it’s more the not being able to settle down. I do have trouble focusing sometimes, but the thing that really made me come in is this feeling like I can’t settle inside my own skin anymore.\n\nIt’s like I have to jump up and do the next thing, even when I’m worn out and I know I should just be sitting still. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but that’s the part that worries me most. Is that answering what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your trouble with settling down is more related to feeling restless or having too much energy?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more restlessness than having extra energy. I don’t feel energetic, really… sometimes I’m actually worn out.\n\nIt’s more like I can’t settle inside my own skin, like I ought to be able to sit still but something in me keeps saying, “Get up, do the next thing.” I know that may sound a little silly, but that’s the best way I can describe it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often experience strong emotions, such as irritability or frustration, when you're unable to engage in activities or move around?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them strong emotions, exactly. I mean… I can get a little frustrated if I feel stuck sitting still, but I don’t know that it turns into anything big.\n\nIt’s more uncomfortable than angry, if that makes sense. Like I’m aware I want to move or do something, and then I feel bothered by not being able to. But I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your trouble paying attention to details is as bothersome to you as your restlessness and inability to settle down?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, maybe not as much as the restlessness, but it does bother me more than I like to admit.\n\nI’ll think I’ve checked something, and then I’ve still missed a little detail. Like with the garden club checkbook, I wrote the wrong amount once, and it turned into a bigger mess than it should have. Or I’ll leave someone’s name off the church supper list even though I truly thought I had gone over it.\n\nIt sounds small when I say it, but then other people have to help fix it, and I feel awful. So yes… it is bothersome, just in a different way than the not being able to settle down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty paying attention to details is a separate issue from your restlessness, or are they connected in some way?\n\nPatient: I think they might be connected, at least some. When I’m feeling unsettled, I probably don’t slow down enough to really check things the way I should.\n\nBut… even when I think I have checked, I still make these little mistakes that turn into bigger messes. Like writing the wrong amount in the garden checkbook, or leaving a name off the church supper list. I really did think I had looked it over, so that part worries me.\n\nI don’t know if that answers it the right way, but it feels like they sort of feed into each other.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Like with the garden club checkbook, I wrote the wrong amount once, and it turned into a bigger mess than it should have. Or I’ll leave someone’s name off the church supper list even though I truly thought I had gone over it.\n\nIt sounds small when I say it, but then other people have to help fix it, and I feel awful. So yes… it is bothersome, just in a different way than the not being able to settle down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty paying attention to details is a separate issue from your restlessness, or are they connected in some way?\n\nPatient: I think they might be connected, at least some. I mean, I like things done a certain way sometimes, but I don’t feel like I have to repeat something over and over or do it just right before I can feel okay. At least I haven’t noticed anything like that. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of restlessness and inability to settle down interfering with your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say it doesn’t, but… yes, I think it has. My family has had to help me more with bills and paperwork because I get unsettled and don’t finish things the way I should. And at church or in the garden group, there have been times people had to redo plans or cover something I said I’d handle and then dropped.\n\nI’m embarrassed saying that, because I do care about those groups very much. I don’t want people thinking I’m unreliable, but I think some of them don’t feel they can count on me like they used to. Is that the kind of interference you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or distressed when you're unable to engage in these activities or move around?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I feel a little silly saying it that way. It’s not that I’m trying to be difficult. I just feel like I can’t settle inside my own skin anymore sometimes, like I need to jump up and do the next thing even when I’m tired and should just be sitting still.\n\nIf I can’t get up or move around, I get uncomfortable and sort of distressed inside. I try to hide it, but it’s there. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to perform specific rituals or behaviors to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I have anything like that. I may double-check things now and then, but not because I think something terrible will happen if I don’t do it a certain way.\n\nI don’t have rituals, at least not that I’m aware of. If I’m misunderstanding what you mean, you can tell me, but I don’t think that’s really me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I don’t like to think of myself that way. It’s more that in conversations, especially committee meetings, a thought pops into my head and I’m afraid I’ll lose it, so I just say it.\n\nSometimes I realize afterward that I interrupted someone who was still talking. I don’t mean to be rude at all, and I feel badly when I notice it. But yes, I do blurt things out before I’ve really thought through whether it’s the right moment. Is that the kind of impulsive thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need to repeat certain actions or tasks over and over?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Not in the sense of having to repeat something over and over until it feels right, or because I’m afraid something will happen.\n\nI may go back to something if I forgot what I was doing, but that feels different to me. I don’t have a strong need to repeat actions like a ritual, at least not that I’ve noticed. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes in your daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’m afraid I do, more than I’d like to admit. I try to be careful, I really do, but I still make these little mistakes that end up causing a bigger problem.\n\nLike I’ll write the wrong amount in the garden checkbook, or I’ll leave someone’s name off the church supper list even though I honestly thought I had checked it. Then later I feel awful, because it looks careless, and I don’t want people to think I don’t care.\n\nSo yes, I guess paying close attention to the details is hard for me sometimes. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these feelings of restlessness and inability to settle down in all areas of your life, or are they mostly limited to specific situations or environments?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s not just one place, I don’t think. I wish I could say it was only when I’m busy or around certain people, but it feels more inside me than outside.\n\nThat’s actually part of why I came in. I feel like I can’t settle in my own skin anymore, like I need to jump up and do the next thing even when I’m tired and should be able to just sit still. I try to make it sound normal, like I’m just keeping busy, but it doesn’t always feel normal to me.\n\nDoes that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is that you can't focus or pay attention, or that you just can't seem to settle down and be still?\n\nPatient: I think… if I’m being honest, it’s more the not being able to settle down. I do have trouble focusing sometimes, but the thing that really made me come in is this feeling like I can’t settle inside my own skin anymore.\n\nIt’s like I have to jump up and do the next thing, even when I’m worn out and I know I should just be sitting still. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but that’s the part that worries me most. Is that answering what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your trouble with settling down is more related to feeling restless or having too much energy?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more restlessness than having extra energy. I don’t feel energetic, really… sometimes I’m actually worn out.\n\nIt’s more like I can’t settle inside my own skin, like I ought to be able to sit still but something in me keeps saying, “Get up, do the next thing.” I know that may sound a little silly, but that’s the best way I can describe it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often experience strong emotions, such as irritability or frustration, when you're unable to engage in activities or move around?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them strong emotions, exactly. I mean… I can get a little frustrated if I feel stuck sitting still, but I don’t know that it turns into anything big.\n\nIt’s more uncomfortable than angry, if that makes sense. Like I’m aware I want to move or do something, and then I feel bothered by not being able to. But I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your trouble paying attention to details is as bothersome to you as your restlessness and inability to settle down?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, maybe not as much as the restlessness, but it does bother me more than I like to admit.\n\nI’ll think I’ve checked something, and then I’ve still missed a little detail. Like with the garden club checkbook, I wrote the wrong amount once, and it turned into a bigger mess than it should have. Or I’ll leave someone’s name off the church supper list even though I truly thought I had gone over it.\n\nIt sounds small when I say it, but then other people have to help fix it, and I feel awful. So yes… it is bothersome, just in a different way than the not being able to settle down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty paying attention to details is a separate issue from your restlessness, or are they connected in some way?\n\nPatient: I think they might be connected, at least some. When I’m feeling unsettled, I probably don’t slow down enough to really check things the way I should.\n\nBut… even when I think I have checked, I still make these little mistakes that turn into bigger messes. Like writing the wrong amount in the garden checkbook, or leaving a name off the church supper list. I really did think I had looked it over, so that part worries me.\n\nI don’t know if that answers it the right way, but it feels like they sort of feed into each other.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make decisions without fully thinking them through?\n\nPatient: I don’t think of myself as someone who makes big decisions without thinking… at least, I hope I don’t.\n\nMaybe with smaller things I might answer too quickly or agree to something before I’ve really sat with it, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean. I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is. Could you give me an example of the kind of decisions you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: When did you first start noticing these feelings of restlessness and inability to settle down?\n\nPatient: I suppose I started really noticing it after my husband died… it’s been about a year and ten months now.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like everything fell apart, but that’s when it began causing real trouble for me. Before that, maybe I was just busy or didn’t think much of it, but since then the restlessness and not being able to settle down have been much harder to ignore. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F90.0", "F90.8"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is. Before that, maybe I was just busy or didn’t think much of it, but since then the restlessness and not being able to settle down have been much harder to ignore. Does that make sense?\n\n[Last Question]\nHow old were you when you first started experiencing these feelings of restlessness and inability to settle down?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 66 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Additional Requirement 1\nmanifestation: \"Even back in grade school, my teachers wrote that I was bright but careless, always losing papers, talking out of turn, and needing reminders to stay in my seat or finish my work.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess… if I’m being honest, it goes back pretty far. Probably grade school age.

I don’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible child or anything, because I did well enough and I was considered bright, but I remember teachers writing things like I was careless, or that I lost papers, talked out of turn, needed reminders to stay in my seat, or to finish my work.

So I suppose those feelings of not settling down were there when I was young, even if I didn’t really understand them that way then. Is that the kind of thing you mean?

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I came in because I just can’t seem to settle down inside myself anymore. It’s like I’m always needing to get up and do the next thing, even when I’m tired and I know I ought to just sit still. I don’t know if that makes sense, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts or ideas that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so, not in the way you’re asking, I don’t believe. I mean, I worry about things like anyone does, and sometimes something will be on my mind, but I wouldn’t say I have the same thought repeating over and over that I can’t get rid of. At least, I haven’t noticed that. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or having trouble sitting still in situations where you're expected to remain seated?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, more than I’d like to admit. Even at church meetings, when I know I’m supposed to just sit quietly, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot or rubbing the edge of my purse, or shifting around in the chair. I try to be discreet about it, but sometimes the ladies beside me start looking over, and then I feel embarrassed. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do certain things in a specific way or repeat certain actions to feel okay?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. I mean, I like things done a certain way sometimes, but I don’t feel like I have to repeat something over and over or do it just right before I can feel okay. At least I haven’t noticed anything like that. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of restlessness and inability to settle down interfering with your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say it doesn’t, but… yes, I think it has. My family has had to help me more with bills and paperwork because I get unsettled and don’t finish things the way I should. And at church or in the garden group, there have been times people had to redo plans or cover something I said I’d handle and then dropped.\n\nI’m embarrassed saying that, because I do care about those groups very much. I don’t want people thinking I’m unreliable, but I think some of them don’t feel they can count on me like they used to. Is that the kind of interference you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or distressed when you're unable to engage in these activities or move around?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I feel a little silly saying it that way. It’s not that I’m trying to be difficult. I just feel like I can’t settle inside my own skin anymore sometimes, like I need to jump up and do the next thing even when I’m tired and should just be sitting still.\n\nIf I can’t get up or move around, I get uncomfortable and sort of distressed inside. I try to hide it, but it’s there. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to perform specific rituals or behaviors to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I have anything like that. I may double-check things now and then, but not because I think something terrible will happen if I don’t do it a certain way.\n\nI don’t have rituals, at least not that I’m aware of. If I’m misunderstanding what you mean, you can tell me, but I don’t think that’s really me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I don’t like to think of myself that way. It’s more that in conversations, especially committee meetings, a thought pops into my head and I’m afraid I’ll lose it, so I just say it.\n\nSometimes I realize afterward that I interrupted someone who was still talking. I don’t mean to be rude at all, and I feel badly when I notice it. But yes, I do blurt things out before I’ve really thought through whether it’s the right moment. Is that the kind of impulsive thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need to repeat certain actions or tasks over and over?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Not in the sense of having to repeat something over and over until it feels right, or because I’m afraid something will happen.\n\nI may go back to something if I forgot what I was doing, but that feels different to me. I don’t have a strong need to repeat actions like a ritual, at least not that I’ve noticed. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes in your daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’m afraid I do, more than I’d like to admit. I try to be careful, I really do, but I still make these little mistakes that end up causing a bigger problem.\n\nLike I’ll write the wrong amount in the garden checkbook, or I’ll leave someone’s name off the church supper list even though I honestly thought I had checked it. Then later I feel awful, because it looks careless, and I don’t want people to think I don’t care.\n\nSo yes, I guess paying close attention to the details is hard for me sometimes. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these feelings of restlessness and inability to settle down in all areas of your life, or are they mostly limited to specific situations or environments?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s not just one place, I don’t think. I wish I could say it was only when I’m busy or around certain people, but it feels more inside me than outside.\n\nThat’s actually part of why I came in. I feel like I can’t settle in my own skin anymore, like I need to jump up and do the next thing even when I’m tired and should be able to just sit still. I try to make it sound normal, like I’m just keeping busy, but it doesn’t always feel normal to me.\n\nDoes that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is that you can't focus or pay attention, or that you just can't seem to settle down and be still?\n\nPatient: I think… if I’m being honest, it’s more the not being able to settle down. I do have trouble focusing sometimes, but the thing that really made me come in is this feeling like I can’t settle inside my own skin anymore.\n\nIt’s like I have to jump up and do the next thing, even when I’m worn out and I know I should just be sitting still. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but that’s the part that worries me most. Is that answering what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your trouble with settling down is more related to feeling restless or having too much energy?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more restlessness than having extra energy. I don’t feel energetic, really… sometimes I’m actually worn out.\n\nIt’s more like I can’t settle inside my own skin, like I ought to be able to sit still but something in me keeps saying, “Get up, do the next thing.” I know that may sound a little silly, but that’s the best way I can describe it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often experience strong emotions, such as irritability or frustration, when you're unable to engage in activities or move around?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them strong emotions, exactly. I mean… I can get a little frustrated if I feel stuck sitting still, but I don’t know that it turns into anything big.\n\nIt’s more uncomfortable than angry, if that makes sense. Like I’m aware I want to move or do something, and then I feel bothered by not being able to. But I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your trouble paying attention to details is as bothersome to you as your restlessness and inability to settle down?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, maybe not as much as the restlessness, but it does bother me more than I like to admit.\n\nI’ll think I’ve checked something, and then I’ve still missed a little detail. Like with the garden club checkbook, I wrote the wrong amount once, and it turned into a bigger mess than it should have. Or I’ll leave someone’s name off the church supper list even though I truly thought I had gone over it.\n\nIt sounds small when I say it, but then other people have to help fix it, and I feel awful. So yes… it is bothersome, just in a different way than the not being able to settle down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty paying attention to details is a separate issue from your restlessness, or are they connected in some way?\n\nPatient: I think they might be connected, at least some. When I’m feeling unsettled, I probably don’t slow down enough to really check things the way I should.\n\nBut… even when I think I have checked, I still make these little mistakes that turn into bigger messes. Like writing the wrong amount in the garden checkbook, or leaving a name off the church supper list. I really did think I had looked it over, so that part worries me.\n\nI don’t know if that answers it the right way, but it feels like they sort of feed into each other.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make decisions without fully thinking them through?\n\nPatient: I don’t think of myself as someone who makes big decisions without thinking… at least, I hope I don’t.\n\nMaybe with smaller things I might answer too quickly or agree to something before I’ve really sat with it, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean. I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is. Could you give me an example of the kind of decisions you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: When did you first start noticing these feelings of restlessness and inability to settle down?\n\nPatient: I suppose I started really noticing it after my husband died… it’s been about a year and ten months now.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like everything fell apart, but that’s when it began causing real trouble for me. Before that, maybe I was just busy or didn’t think much of it, but since then the restlessness and not being able to settle down have been much harder to ignore. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: How old were you when you first started experiencing these feelings of restlessness and inability to settle down?\n\nPatient: I guess… if I’m being honest, it goes back pretty far. Probably grade school age.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible child or anything, because I did well enough and I was considered bright, but I remember teachers writing things like I was careless, or that I lost papers, talked out of turn, needed reminders to stay in my seat, or to finish my work.\n\nSo I suppose those feelings of not settling down were there when I was young, even if I didn’t really understand them that way then. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

(Note: In adolescents or adults, may be limited to feeling restless.)\n  manifestation: I came in because I feel like I cannot settle inside my own skin anymore, like I have to jump up and do the next manifestation: Quiet things I used to enjoy, like sitting with a quilt pattern or reading my Bible, don’t stay quiet for long because I start humming, talking to myself, getting up, and fussing with something else.\n- key: S014\n  name: Constant_Activity\n  description: Is often 'on the go', acting as if 'driven by a motor'.\n  manifestation: Most days I move from the garden to the senior center to church errands to sorting papers at home without taking a real breath, as if stopping would make everything fall apart.\n- key: S017\n  name: Waiting_Turn_Difficulty\n  description: Often has difficulty waiting his or her turn.\n  manifestation: Waiting my turn makes me so edgy that at the clinic desk or after choir practice I catch myself stepping in, sighing, or speaking before the person ahead of me is finished.\n- key: S018\n  name: Interrupting_Intruding\n  description: Often interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n  manifestation: I interrupt people more than I mean to, especially in committee meetings, because a thought pops into my head and I blurt it out before I lose it, even when someone else is still talking.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Even back in grade school, my teachers wrote that I was bright I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is. Could you give me an example of the kind of decisions you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: When did you first start noticing these feelings of restlessness and inability to settle down?\n\nPatient: I suppose I started really noticing it after my husband died… it’s been about a year and ten months now.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like everything fell apart, but that’s when it began causing real trouble for me. Before that, maybe I was just busy or didn’t think much of it, but since then the restlessness and not being able to settle down have been much harder to ignore. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: How old were you when you first started experiencing these feelings of restlessness and inability to settle down?\n\nPatient: I guess… if I’m being honest, it goes back pretty far. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think you've always had some level of difficulty with paying attention or sitting still, even as a child?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Yes… I think so. I don’t want to overstate it, because I was always told I was bright and I did manage, but even in grade school my teachers wrote things like I was careless, or that I was always losing papers.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I came in because I just can’t seem to settle down inside myself anymore. It’s like I’m always needing to get up and do the next thing, even when I’m tired and I know I ought to just sit still. I don’t know if that makes sense, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts or ideas that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so, not in the way you’re asking, I don’t believe. I mean, I worry about things like anyone does, and sometimes something will be on my mind, but I wouldn’t say I have the same thought repeating over and over that I can’t get rid of. At least, I haven’t noticed that. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or having trouble sitting still in situations where you're expected to remain seated?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, more than I’d like to admit. Even at church meetings, when I know I’m supposed to just sit quietly, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot or rubbing the edge of my purse, or shifting around in the chair. I try to be discreet about it, but sometimes the ladies beside me start looking over, and then I feel embarrassed. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do certain things in a specific way or repeat certain actions to feel okay?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. I mean, I like things done a certain way sometimes, but I don’t feel like I have to repeat something over and over or do it just right before I can feel okay. At least I haven’t noticed anything like that. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of restlessness and inability to settle down interfering with your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say it doesn’t, but… yes, I think it has. My family has had to help me more with bills and paperwork because I get unsettled and don’t finish things the way I should. And at church or in the garden group, there have been times people had to redo plans or cover something I said I’d handle and then dropped.\n\nI’m embarrassed saying that, because I do care about those groups very much. I don’t want people thinking I’m unreliable, but I think some of them don’t feel they can count on me like they used to. Is that the kind of interference you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or distressed when you're unable to engage in these activities or move around?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I feel a little silly saying it that way. It’s not that I’m trying to be difficult. I just feel like I can’t settle inside my own skin anymore sometimes, like I need to jump up and do the next thing even when I’m tired and should just be sitting still.\n\nIf I can’t get up or move around, I get uncomfortable and sort of distressed inside. I try to hide it, but it’s there. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to perform specific rituals or behaviors to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I have anything like that. I may double-check things now and then, but not because I think something terrible will happen if I don’t do it a certain way.\n\nI don’t have rituals, at least not that I’m aware of. If I’m misunderstanding what you mean, you can tell me, but I don’t think that’s really me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I don’t like to think of myself that way. It’s more that in conversations, especially committee meetings, a thought pops into my head and I’m afraid I’ll lose it, so I just say it.\n\nSometimes I realize afterward that I interrupted someone who was still talking. I don’t mean to be rude at all, and I feel badly when I notice it. But yes, I do blurt things out before I’ve really thought through whether it’s the right moment. Is that the kind of impulsive thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need to repeat certain actions or tasks over and over?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Not in the sense of having to repeat something over and over until it feels right, or because I’m afraid something will happen.\n\nI may go back to something if I forgot what I was doing, but that feels different to me. I don’t have a strong need to repeat actions like a ritual, at least not that I’ve noticed. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes in your daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’m afraid I do, more than I’d like to admit. I try to be careful, I really do, but I still make these little mistakes that end up causing a bigger problem.\n\nLike I’ll write the wrong amount in the garden checkbook, or I’ll leave someone’s name off the church supper list even though I honestly thought I had checked it. Then later I feel awful, because it looks careless, and I don’t want people to think I don’t care.\n\nSo yes, I guess paying close attention to the details is hard for me sometimes. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these feelings of restlessness and inability to settle down in all areas of your life, or are they mostly limited to specific situations or environments?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s not just one place, I don’t think. I wish I could say it was only when I’m busy or around certain people, but it feels more inside me than outside.\n\nThat’s actually part of why I came in. I feel like I can’t settle in my own skin anymore, like I need to jump up and do the next thing even when I’m tired and should be able to just sit still. I try to make it sound normal, like I’m just keeping busy, but it doesn’t always feel normal to me.\n\nDoes that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is that you can't focus or pay attention, or that you just can't seem to settle down and be still?\n\nPatient: I think… if I’m being honest, it’s more the not being able to settle down. I do have trouble focusing sometimes, but the thing that really made me come in is this feeling like I can’t settle inside my own skin anymore.\n\nIt’s like I have to jump up and do the next thing, even when I’m worn out and I know I should just be sitting still. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but that’s the part that worries me most. Is that answering what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your trouble with settling down is more related to feeling restless or having too much energy?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more restlessness than having extra energy. I don’t feel energetic, really… sometimes I’m actually worn out.\n\nIt’s more like I can’t settle inside my own skin, like I ought to be able to sit still but something in me keeps saying, “Get up, do the next thing.” I know that may sound a little silly, but that’s the best way I can describe it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often experience strong emotions, such as irritability or frustration, when you're unable to engage in activities or move around?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them strong emotions, exactly. I mean… I can get a little frustrated if I feel stuck sitting still, but I don’t know that it turns into anything big.\n\nIt’s more uncomfortable than angry, if that makes sense. Like I’m aware I want to move or do something, and then I feel bothered by not being able to. But I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your trouble paying attention to details is as bothersome to you as your restlessness and inability to settle down?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, maybe not as much as the restlessness, but it does bother me more than I like to admit.\n\nI’ll think I’ve checked something, and then I’ve still missed a little detail. Like with the garden club checkbook, I wrote the wrong amount once, and it turned into a bigger mess than it should have. Or I’ll leave someone’s name off the church supper list even though I truly thought I had gone over it.\n\nIt sounds small when I say it, but then other people have to help fix it, and I feel awful. So yes… it is bothersome, just in a different way than the not being able to settle down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty paying attention to details is a separate issue from your restlessness, or are they connected in some way?\n\nPatient: I think they might be connected, at least some. When I’m feeling unsettled, I probably don’t slow down enough to really check things the way I should.\n\nBut… even when I think I have checked, I still make these little mistakes that turn into bigger messes. Like writing the wrong amount in the garden checkbook, or leaving a name off the church supper list. I really did think I had looked it over, so that part worries me.\n\nI don’t know if that answers it the right way, but it feels like they sort of feed into each other.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make decisions without fully thinking them through?\n\nPatient: I don’t think of myself as someone who makes big decisions without thinking… at least, I hope I don’t.\n\nMaybe with smaller things I might answer too quickly or agree to something before I’ve really sat with it, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean. I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is. Could you give me an example of the kind of decisions you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: When did you first start noticing these feelings of restlessness and inability to settle down?\n\nPatient: I suppose I started really noticing it after my husband died… it’s been about a year and ten months now.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like everything fell apart, but that’s when it began causing real trouble for me. Before that, maybe I was just busy or didn’t think much of it, but since then the restlessness and not being able to settle down have been much harder to ignore. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: How old were you when you first started experiencing these feelings of restlessness and inability to settle down?\n\nPatient: I guess… if I’m being honest, it goes back pretty far. Probably grade school age.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible child or anything, because I did well enough and I was considered bright, but I remember teachers writing things like I was careless, or that I lost papers, talked out of turn, needed reminders to stay in my seat, or to finish my work.\n\nSo I suppose those feelings of not settling down were there when I was young, even if I didn’t really understand them that way then. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've always had some level of difficulty with paying attention or sitting still, even as a child?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to overstate it, because I was always told I was bright and I did manage, but even in grade school my teachers wrote things like I was careless, or that I was always losing papers.\n\nThey’d also say I talked out of turn and needed reminders to stay in my seat or finish my work. So… yes, I suppose there was some difficulty with attention and sitting still even then. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
