Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these feelings of restlessness and inability to settle down in all areas of your life, or are they mostly limited to specific situations or environments?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s not just one place, I don’t think. I wish I could say it was only when I’m busy or around certain people, but it feels more inside me than outside.\n\nThat’s actually part of why I came in. I feel like I can’t settle in my own skin anymore, like I need to jump up and do the next thing even when I’m tired and should be able to just sit still. I try to make it sound normal, like I’m just keeping busy, but it doesn’t always feel normal to me.\n\nDoes that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is that you can't focus or pay attention, or that you just can't seem to settle down and be still?\n\nPatient: I think… if I’m being honest, it’s more the not being able to settle down. I do have trouble focusing sometimes, but the thing that really made me come in is this feeling like I can’t settle inside my own skin anymore.\n\nIt’s like I have to jump up and do the next thing, even when I’m worn out and I know I should just be sitting still. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but that’s the part that worries me most. Is that answering what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your trouble with settling down is more related to feeling restless or having too much energy?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more restlessness than having extra energy. I don’t feel energetic, really… sometimes I’m actually worn out.\n\nIt’s more like I can’t settle inside my own skin, like I ought to be able to sit still but something in me keeps saying, “Get up, do the next thing.” I know that may sound a little silly, but that’s the best way I can describe it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often experience strong emotions, such as irritability or frustration, when you're unable to engage in activities or move around?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them strong emotions, exactly. I mean… I can get a little frustrated if I feel stuck sitting still, but I don’t know that it turns into anything big.\n\nIt’s more uncomfortable than angry, if that makes sense. Like I’m aware I want to move or do something, and then I feel bothered by not being able to. But I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your trouble paying attention to details is as bothersome to you as your restlessness and inability to settle down?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, maybe not as much as the restlessness, but it does bother me more than I like to admit.\n\nI’ll think I’ve checked something, and then I’ve still missed a little detail. Like with the garden club checkbook, I wrote the wrong amount once, and it turned into a bigger mess than it should have. Or I’ll leave someone’s name off the church supper list even though I truly thought I had gone over it.\n\nIt sounds small when I say it, but then other people have to help fix it, and I feel awful. So yes… it is bothersome, just in a different way than the not being able to settle down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty paying attention to details is a separate issue from your restlessness, or are they connected in some way?\n\nPatient: I think they might be connected, at least some. When I’m feeling unsettled, I probably don’t slow down enough to really check things the way I should.\n\nBut… even when I think I have checked, I still make these little mistakes that turn into bigger messes. Like writing the wrong amount in the garden checkbook, or leaving a name off the church supper list. I really did think I had looked it over, so that part worries me.\n\nI don’t know if that answers it the right way, but it feels like they sort of feed into each other.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make decisions without fully thinking them through?\n\nPatient: I don’t think of myself as someone who makes big decisions without thinking… at least, I hope I don’t.\n\nMaybe with smaller things I might answer too quickly or agree to something before I’ve really sat with it, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean. I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is. Could you give me an example of the kind of decisions you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: When did you first start noticing these feelings of restlessness and inability to settle down?\n\nPatient: I suppose I started really noticing it after my husband died… it’s been about a year and ten months now.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like everything fell apart, but that’s when it began causing real trouble for me. Before that, maybe I was just busy or didn’t think much of it, but since then the restlessness and not being able to settle down have been much harder to ignore. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: How old were you when you first started experiencing these feelings of restlessness and inability to settle down?\n\nPatient: I guess… if I’m being honest, it goes back pretty far. Probably grade school age.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible child or anything, because I did well enough and I was considered bright, but I remember teachers writing things like I was careless, or that I lost papers, talked out of turn, needed reminders to stay in my seat, or to finish my work.\n\nSo I suppose those feelings of not settling down were there when I was young, even if I didn’t really understand them that way then. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've always had some level of difficulty with paying attention or sitting still, even as a child?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to overstate it, because I was always told I was bright and I did manage, but even in grade school my teachers wrote things like I was careless, or that I was always losing papers.\n\nThey’d also say I talked out of turn and needed reminders to stay in my seat or finish my work. So… yes, I suppose there was some difficulty with attention and sitting still even then. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?"
